% Make \paragraph and \subparagraph free-standing
\makeatletter
\ifx\paragraph\undefined\else
  \let\oldparagraph\paragraph
  \renewcommand{\paragraph}{
    \@ifstar
      \xxxParagraphStar
      \xxxParagraphNoStar
  }
  \newcommand{\xxxParagraphStar}[1]{\oldparagraph*{#1}\mbox{}}
  \newcommand{\xxxParagraphNoStar}[1]{\oldparagraph{#1}\mbox{}}
\fi
\ifx\subparagraph\undefined\else
  \let\oldsubparagraph\subparagraph
  \renewcommand{\subparagraph}{
    \@ifstar
      \xxxSubParagraphStar
      \xxxSubParagraphNoStar
  }
  \newcommand{\xxxSubParagraphStar}[1]{\oldsubparagraph*{#1}\mbox{}}
  \newcommand{\xxxSubParagraphNoStar}[1]{\oldsubparagraph{#1}\mbox{}}
\fi
\makeatother

\usepackage{graphicx}
\makeatletter
\newsavebox\pandoc@box
\newcommand*\pandocbounded[1]{% scales image to fit in text height/width
  \sbox\pandoc@box{#1}%
  \Gscale@div\@tempa{\textheight}{\dimexpr\ht\pandoc@box+\dp\pandoc@box\relax}%
  \Gscale@div\@tempb{\linewidth}{\wd\pandoc@box}%
  \ifdim\@tempb\p@<\@tempa\p@\let\@tempa\@tempb\fi% select the smaller of both
  \ifdim\@tempa\p@<\p@\scalebox{\@tempa}{\usebox\pandoc@box}%
  \else\usebox{\pandoc@box}%
  \fi%
}

\NewDocumentCommand\citeproctext{}{}

  \begingroup\def\citeproctext{#2}\cite{#1}\endgroup}

 % allow citations to break across lines

\newlength{\cslhangindent}
\setlength{\cslhangindent}{1.5em}
\newlength{\csllabelwidth}
\setlength{\csllabelwidth}{3em}
\newenvironment{CSLReferences}[2] % #1 hanging-indent, #2 entry-spacing
 {\begin{list}{}{%
  \setlength{\itemindent}{0pt}
  \setlength{\leftmargin}{0pt}
  \setlength{\parsep}{0pt}
  % turn on hanging indent if param 1 is 1
  \ifodd #1
   \setlength{\leftmargin}{\cslhangindent}
   \setlength{\itemindent}{-1\cslhangindent}
  \fi
  % set entry spacing
  \setlength{\itemsep}{#2\baselineskip}}}
 {\end{list}}
\usepackage{calc}
\newcommand{\CSLBlock}[1]{\hfill\break\parbox[t]{\linewidth}{\strut\ignorespaces#1\strut}}
\newcommand{\CSLLeftMargin}[1]{\parbox[t]{\csllabelwidth}{\strut#1\strut}}
\newcommand{\CSLRightInline}[1]{\parbox[t]{\linewidth - \csllabelwidth}{\strut#1\strut}}
\newcommand{\CSLIndent}[1]{\hspace{\cslhangindent}#1}



\setlength{\emergencystretch}{3em} % prevent overfull lines

  \setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}\setlength{\parskip}{0pt}}



 


% =============================================================================
% FTD MANUSCRIPT v2 — ELITE PHYSICS TEXTBOOK TYPOGRAPHY
% =============================================================================
% Design language: Gold = achievement, Darkblue = authority,
% Purple = foundational (axiom), Blue = proven (theorem),
% Teal = reference (definition), Gold-dashed = argued (selection),
% Orange = speculative (conjecture)
% =============================================================================

% --- FONTS ---
% LuaLaTeX uses Latin Modern by default (excellent math support)
% mathpazo (Palatino) requires pdflatex; TexLive repo currently broken
% so we use LM which renders ALL math symbols correctly including \ast, \Gamma, \pi
\usepackage{microtype}        % Optical margin alignment, font expansion

% --- COLOR SYSTEM ---
\usepackage{xcolor}

% Primary palette (from The Golden Thread)
\definecolor{goldthread}{RGB}{201, 162, 39}
\definecolor{darkblue}{RGB}{44, 62, 80}

% Epistemic callout colors
\definecolor{axiomPurple}{RGB}{93, 33, 182}
\definecolor{axiomBg}{RGB}{250, 248, 255}
\definecolor{theoremBlue}{RGB}{37, 99, 235}
\definecolor{theoremBg}{RGB}{248, 250, 255}
\definecolor{definitionTeal}{RGB}{13, 148, 136}
\definecolor{definitionBg}{RGB}{248, 253, 252}
\definecolor{selectionGold}{RGB}{202, 138, 4}
\definecolor{selectionBg}{RGB}{255, 254, 245}
\definecolor{conjectureBurn}{RGB}{234, 88, 12}
\definecolor{conjectureBg}{RGB}{255, 250, 245}
\definecolor{warningRed}{RGB}{220, 38, 38}
\definecolor{tableStripe}{RGB}{245, 245, 250}

% Override Quarto's default callout colors
\definecolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{RGB}{44, 62, 80}
\definecolor{quarto-callout-important-color}{RGB}{220, 38, 38}
\definecolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{RGB}{234, 88, 12}
\definecolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{RGB}{13, 148, 136}
\definecolor{quarto-callout-caution-color}{RGB}{202, 138, 4}

% --- CALLOUT BOXES (tcolorbox) ---
\usepackage[most]{tcolorbox}
\tcbuselibrary{skins, breakable}

% AXIOM: purple left bar, light purple background
\newtcolorbox{ftdaxiom}[1][]{%
  enhanced, breakable,
  colback=axiomBg,
  colframe=axiomPurple,
  coltitle=axiomPurple,
  fonttitle=\bfseries\scshape,
  title={#1},
  left=4mm, right=4mm, top=3mm, bottom=3mm,
  borderline west={3pt}{0pt}{axiomPurple},
  frame hidden, boxrule=0pt, sharp corners,
  before upper={\setlength{\parindent}{0pt}},
}

% THEOREM: gold top rule, white body, blue title
\newtcolorbox{ftdtheorem}[1][]{%
  enhanced, breakable,
  colback=white,
  colframe=theoremBlue,
  coltitle=theoremBlue,
  fonttitle=\bfseries,
  title={#1},
  left=4mm, right=4mm, top=3mm, bottom=3mm,
  borderline north={2pt}{0pt}{goldthread},
  frame hidden, boxrule=0pt, sharp corners,
  before upper={\setlength{\parindent}{0pt}},
}

% DEFINITION: teal left bar + thin border
\newtcolorbox{ftddefinition}[1][]{%
  enhanced, breakable,
  colback=definitionBg,
  colframe=definitionTeal,
  coltitle=definitionTeal,
  fonttitle=\bfseries,
  title={#1},
  left=4mm, right=4mm, top=3mm, bottom=3mm,
  borderline west={3pt}{0pt}{definitionTeal},
  borderline={0.5pt}{0pt}{definitionTeal!30},
  sharp corners,
  before upper={\setlength{\parindent}{0pt}},
}

% SELECTION: dashed gold border (signals "not fully proven")
\newtcolorbox{ftdselection}[1][]{%
  enhanced, breakable,
  colback=selectionBg,
  colframe=selectionGold,
  coltitle=selectionGold!80!black,
  fonttitle=\bfseries,
  title={#1},
  left=4mm, right=4mm, top=3mm, bottom=3mm,
  borderline={1pt}{0pt}{goldthread, dashed},
  sharp corners,
  before upper={\setlength{\parindent}{0pt}},
}

% CONJECTURE: orange left bar, italic label, warm background
\newtcolorbox{ftdconjecture}[1][]{%
  enhanced, breakable,
  colback=conjectureBg,
  colframe=conjectureBurn,
  coltitle=conjectureBurn,
  fonttitle=\bfseries\itshape,
  title={#1},
  left=4mm, right=4mm, top=3mm, bottom=3mm,
  borderline west={3pt}{0pt}{conjectureBurn},
  frame hidden, boxrule=0pt, sharp corners,
  before upper={\setlength{\parindent}{0pt}},
}

% --- CHAPTER TITLE DESIGN (titlesec) ---
\usepackage{titlesec}

% Chapter: gold number on own line, darkblue title, gold rule below
% raggedright prevents hyphenation; shrink font slightly to fit long titles
\titleformat{\chapter}[display]
  {\normalfont\huge\bfseries\raggedright}
  {\textcolor{goldthread}{\fontsize{60}{72}\selectfont\thechapter}}
  {10pt}
  {\color{darkblue}\LARGE\raggedright}
  [\vspace{4pt}{\color{goldthread}\titlerule[1.5pt]}]

\titlespacing*{\chapter}{0pt}{40pt}{30pt}

% Part: centered, gold label, darkblue name
\titleformat{\part}[display]
  {\centering\normalfont}
  {\textcolor{goldthread}{\fontsize{14}{16}\selectfont\scshape\partname\ \thepart}}
  {12pt}
  {\fontsize{22}{26}\selectfont\color{darkblue}\bfseries}
  [\vspace{8pt}{\color{goldthread}\titlerule[1.5pt]}]

% ALL headings: raggedright (no hyphenation), no breaks after heading
\titleformat{\section}
  {\normalfont\Large\bfseries\color{darkblue}\raggedright}
  {\thesection}{1em}{}

\titleformat{\subsection}
  {\normalfont\large\bfseries\color{darkblue}\raggedright}
  {\thesubsection}{1em}{}

\titleformat{\subsubsection}
  {\normalfont\normalsize\bfseries\color{darkblue}\raggedright}
  {\thesubsubsection}{1em}{}

% NEVER orphan a heading — force at least 4 lines after any heading
\titlespacing*{\section}{0pt}{1.5ex plus 1ex minus .2ex}{0.8ex plus .2ex}
\titlespacing*{\subsection}{0pt}{1.25ex plus 1ex minus .2ex}{0.6ex plus .2ex}
\titlespacing*{\subsubsection}{0pt}{1ex plus 1ex minus .2ex}{0.4ex plus .2ex}

% Prevent orphaned headings — require substantial content below
\usepackage{needspace}
\makeatletter
\let\orig@section\section
\renewcommand{\section}{\needspace{8\baselineskip}\orig@section}
\let\orig@subsection\subsection
\renewcommand{\subsection}{\needspace{6\baselineskip}\orig@subsection}
\let\orig@subsubsection\subsubsection
\renewcommand{\subsubsection}{\needspace{5\baselineskip}\orig@subsubsection}
\makeatother

% --- HEADERS AND FOOTERS (fancyhdr) ---
\usepackage{fancyhdr}
\pagestyle{fancy}
\fancyhf{}

% Even pages: book title (left header)
\fancyhead[LE]{\small\scshape\textcolor{darkblue}{Foundational Ternary Dynamics}}
% Odd pages: section name (right header)
\fancyhead[RO]{\small\scshape\textcolor{darkblue}{\nouppercase{\rightmark}}}
% Page numbers in outer footer
\fancyfoot[LE,RO]{\textcolor{darkblue}{\thepage}}

% Gold header rule
\renewcommand{\headrulewidth}{0.4pt}
\renewcommand{\headrule}{\hbox to\headwidth{%
  \color{goldthread}\leaders\hrule height \headrulewidth\hfill}}
\renewcommand{\footrulewidth}{0pt}

% Plain style for chapter-opening pages
\fancypagestyle{plain}{%
  \fancyhf{}
  \fancyfoot[C]{\textcolor{darkblue}{\thepage}}
  \renewcommand{\headrulewidth}{0pt}
}

% --- TABLE DESIGN ---
\usepackage{booktabs}
\usepackage{longtable}
\usepackage{array}
\usepackage{colortbl}

% Gold top/bottom rules, subtle midrules
\arrayrulecolor{darkblue!30}

% Professional captions
\usepackage{caption}
\captionsetup{
  font={small, it},
  labelfont={bf, color=darkblue},
  labelsep=period,
  skip=6pt,
}
\captionsetup[table]{position=below}
\captionsetup[figure]{position=below}

% --- MATH ENHANCEMENT ---
\usepackage{amssymb}
\usepackage{mathtools}

% Gold QED symbol — requires amsthm which Quarto may not load
% \renewcommand{\qedsymbol}{$\textcolor{goldthread}{\blacksquare}$}

% --- DROP CAPS ---
\usepackage{lettrine}
\setcounter{DefaultLines}{2}
\renewcommand{\DefaultLoversize}{0.1}
\renewcommand{\DefaultLraise}{0}
\renewcommand{\DefaultLhang}{0.15}
\renewcommand{\LettrineFontHook}{\color{darkblue}\bfseries}

% --- EPIGRAPH SUPPORT ---
\usepackage{epigraph}
\setlength{\epigraphwidth}{0.75\textwidth}
\renewcommand{\epigraphflush}{center}
\renewcommand{\sourceflush}{center}
\renewcommand{\epigraphrule}{0pt}

% --- LEMNISCATE SECTION SEPARATOR ---
\newcommand{\sectionbreak}{%
  \vspace{1.5em}%
  \begin{center}%
    \textcolor{goldthread}{\Large$\infty$}%
  \end{center}%
  \vspace{1.5em}%
}

% --- PARAGRAPH STYLE: indent, no skip (academic book convention) ---
\setlength{\parindent}{1.5em}
\setlength{\parskip}{0pt plus 1pt}

% --- NO HYPHENATION IN HEADINGS, BOLD, OR TABLE HEADERS ---
% Headings use \raggedright (set in titlesec below)
% Bold text: suppress hyphenation globally in \bfseries contexts
\usepackage{ragged2e}

% Table first-column minimum width to prevent tag hyphenation
\usepackage{tabularx}

% --- STRIP EPISTEMIC TAGS FROM RUNNING HEADERS ---
\renewcommand{\sectionmark}[1]{\markright{#1}}

% --- TYPOGRAPHY CONTROLS ---
\widowpenalty=10000
\clubpenalty=10000
\hyphenpenalty=500
\tolerance=1000

% Float placement
\renewcommand{\floatpagefraction}{0.8}
\renewcommand{\topfraction}{0.9}
\renewcommand{\bottomfraction}{0.9}
\renewcommand{\textfraction}{0.1}

% --- ELIMINATE EXCESSIVE WHITESPACE ---
% Allow LaTeX to stretch/shrink vertical glue to fill pages
\raggedbottom

% Reduce space before chapters (from 40pt to 20pt)
\titlespacing*{\chapter}{0pt}{20pt}{20pt}

% Allow text to flow continuously — no forced page breaks between sections
\renewcommand{\cleardoublepage}{\clearpage}

% Clean lists
\usepackage{enumitem}
\setlist{itemsep=2pt, parsep=2pt}

% --- TABLE OF CONTENTS ---
\usepackage{tocloft}
\setlength{\cftchapnumwidth}{2.5em}
\setlength{\cftsecnumwidth}{3.0em}
\setlength{\cftsubsecnumwidth}{3.8em}
\renewcommand{\cftchapfont}{\bfseries\color{darkblue}}
\renewcommand{\cftchappagefont}{\bfseries\color{darkblue}}
\renewcommand{\cftsecfont}{\color{darkblue!80}}
\renewcommand{\cftsecpagefont}{\color{darkblue!80}}

% --- HYPERREF CONFIGURATION (deferred until hyperref is loaded) ---
\AtBeginDocument{%
  \hypersetup{
    colorlinks=true,
    linkcolor=darkblue,
    urlcolor=goldthread,
    citecolor=darkblue!80,
    pdftitle={Foundational Ternary Dynamics},
    pdfsubject={Deriving the Standard Model from Five Lattice Postulates},
  }%
}

% --- UTILITY COMMANDS ---
\newcommand{\keynumber}[1]{\textbf{\textcolor{darkblue}{#1}}}

% G* rendering: single command, use \Gs everywhere
% Trailing {} prevents eating the next character
\newcommand{\Gs}{G^{*}{}}
\newcommand{\Gssq}{G^{*2}{}}      % G*^2
\newcommand{\Gscu}{G^{*3}{}}      % G*^3

% Afterpage for title page background cleanup
\usepackage{afterpage}
% Titlepage Preamble - Elite Physics Textbook Design
% =============================================================================
% Uses the goldthread/darkblue palette from the main preamble.
% No author name displayed. Title + subtitle + epigraph + year.
% =============================================================================

% Required packages for title page
\usepackage{graphicx}
\usepackage{tikz}
\usepackage{eso-pic}

% Parchment background for title page only
\definecolor{parchment}{RGB}{253,245,230}

% Ornamental line command (gold dots and rules)
\newcommand{\ornamentalline}{%
  \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzpicture}
      \draw[goldthread, line width=0.5pt] (0,0) -- (2,0);
      \fill[goldthread] (2.2,0) circle (2pt);
      \draw[goldthread, line width=0.5pt] (2.4,0) -- (4.4,0);
      \fill[goldthread] (4.6,0) circle (2pt);
      \draw[goldthread, line width=0.5pt] (4.8,0) -- (6.8,0);
    \end{tikzpicture}
  \end{center}
}

% Custom title page
\makeatletter
\renewcommand{\maketitle}{%
  \begin{titlepage}
    \newgeometry{top=1in,bottom=1in,left=1.25in,right=1in}

    % Background color - subtle parchment
    \pagecolor{parchment}

    \begin{center}
      % Top ornamental border
      \vspace*{0.3in}
      \begin{tikzpicture}
        \draw[goldthread, line width=0.8pt] (0,0) rectangle (4in,0.015in);
      \end{tikzpicture}

      \vspace{0.3in}

      % Lemniscate-Alpha Logo
      \IfFileExists{media/images/fig-lemniscate-alpha-logo.png}{%
        \includegraphics[width=1.5in]{media/images/fig-lemniscate-alpha-logo.png}%
      }{%
        % Fallback: lemniscate symbol if logo not found
        \textcolor{goldthread}{\fontsize{48}{56}\selectfont$\infty$}%
      }

      \vspace{0.25in}

      % Ornamental line
      \ornamentalline

      \vspace{0.15in}

      % Title
      {\fontsize{24}{28}\selectfont\bfseries\color{darkblue}%
        FOUNDATIONAL\\[0.08in]
        TERNARY\\[0.08in]
        DYNAMICS}

      \vspace{0.2in}

      % Ornamental line
      \ornamentalline

      \vspace{0.15in}

      % Subtitle
      {\fontsize{11}{14}\selectfont\itshape\color{darkblue!70}%
        Deriving the Standard Model from Five Lattice Postulates}

      \vspace{0.4in}

      % Epigraph
      {\fontsize{9}{12}\selectfont\color{darkblue!60}%
        \textit{``From the void emerges manifestation;\\[2pt]
        from manifestation, structure;\\[2pt]
        from structure, cosmos.''}}

      \vfill

      % Year with ornaments (no author)
      \begin{tikzpicture}
        \draw[goldthread, line width=0.5pt] (0,0) -- (1.2,0);
        \node[darkblue] at (1.7,0) {\fontsize{11}{13}\selectfont\bfseries 2026};
        \draw[goldthread, line width=0.5pt] (2.2,0) -- (3.4,0);
      \end{tikzpicture}

      \vspace{0.3in}

      % Bottom ornamental border
      \begin{tikzpicture}
        \draw[goldthread, line width=0.8pt] (0,0) rectangle (4in,0.015in);
      \end{tikzpicture}

    \end{center}

    % Reset page color
    \afterpage{\nopagecolor}

    \restoregeometry
  \end{titlepage}
}

\chapter*{Foundational Ternary
Dynamics}\label{foundational-ternary-dynamics}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Foundational Ternary Dynamics}

\markboth{Foundational Ternary Dynamics}{Foundational Ternary Dynamics}

\section*{A Discrete Ontology for the Standard Model and
Gravity}\label{a-discrete-ontology-for-the-standard-model-and-gravity}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{A Discrete Ontology for the Standard
Model and Gravity}

\markright{A Discrete Ontology for the Standard Model and Gravity}

This manuscript presents Foundational Ternary Dynamics (FTD): a
computational framework that derives the Standard Model gauge groups,
coupling constants, and particle masses from five postulates about a
discrete cubic lattice.

The framework postulates a three-dimensional integer lattice
\(\mathbb{Z}^3\) where each site carries a ternary state
\(s \in \{-1, 0, +1\}\) and a continuous flux vector
\(\mathbf{J} \in \mathbb{R}^3\). From the lattice geometry alone ---
specifically, the decomposition of the 26-neighbor Moore neighborhood
into three sublattices (SC, FCC, BCC) --- the gauge group
\(U(1) \times SU(2) \times SU(3)\), the fine structure constant
\(\alpha = 1/137.036\) (to 1.26 ppm at tree level, 9.6 ppb with one-loop
correction), and the complete mass spectrum emerge with zero free
parameters.

\subsection*{What this manuscript does}\label{what-this-manuscript-does}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{What this manuscript does}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Derives} the Standard Model gauge groups from lattice geometry
  (Book I)
\item
  \textbf{Computes} 46 observables with zero free parameters (Book II)
\item
  \textbf{Recovers} full nonlinear general relativity from discrete
  dynamics (Book II)
\item
  \textbf{Resolves} the measurement problem via algebraic type
  transition (Book III)
\item
  \textbf{Predicts} dark matter properties, vacuum energy, and proton
  decay (Book III)
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{What this manuscript does not
do}\label{what-this-manuscript-does-not-do}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{What this manuscript does not do}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Claim a theory of everything. FTD is a framework with explicit scope
  limitations.
\item
  Derive quark masses from first principles (scheme-dependent; see Ch.
  13).
\item
  Prove confinement rigorously (see Ch. 18 for the current status).
\item
  Bypass the lattice's violation of continuous diffeomorphism invariance
  (see Ch. 15).
\end{itemize}

\chapter*{Preface}\label{preface}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Preface}

\markboth{Preface}{Preface}

This work began as a computational experiment: simulate a discrete
lattice with ternary states and see what emerges. What emerged was
unexpected.

The 26-neighbor Moore neighborhood on \(\mathbb{Z}^3\) decomposes into
three sublattices --- simple cubic (6 face-adjacent), face-centered
cubic (12 edge-adjacent), and body-centered cubic (8 corner-adjacent).
Each sublattice excites a different number of flux components,
generating exactly the Standard Model gauge group
\(U(1) \times SU(2) \times SU(3)\). The BCC sublattice's eigenvalue ---
a product of cosines, \(1 - \cos k_1 \cos k_2 \cos k_3\) --- connects
through Watson's 1939 integral identity to the lemniscatic constant
\(G^* = \Gamma(1/4)^2/(\sqrt{2}\pi)\). A self-consistency gap equation
with coefficient 16 (the square of the CM curve automorphism count) then
produces two roots: \(1/\alpha = 137.036\) and \(N_c = 3\). Everything
else follows.

The framework has five postulates, six derived constants, and zero free
parameters. It computes 46 Standard Model observables, recovers the full
nonlinear Einstein equations, and resolves the measurement problem
through an algebraic type transition (Type III\(_1\) to Type I) that
requires no external observer.

This manuscript is written for working physicists. It assumes
familiarity with quantum field theory, general relativity, and lattice
gauge theory. Motivation and philosophy are minimal; derivations are
explicit. Every claim carries an epistemic tag indicating whether it is
a theorem, a selection principle, a conjecture, or an open question.

The author makes no claim that this is the final theory of physics. It
is a framework that produces the right numbers from discrete geometry.
Whether it is the \emph{right} framework --- or merely a remarkably
efficient parametrization --- is for the reader to judge. The
falsification criteria are in Chapter 24.

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, breakable, bottomrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, arc=.35mm, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, opacityback=0, bottomtitle=1mm, toptitle=1mm, titlerule=0mm, left=2mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Methodology}, leftrule=.75mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6]

This work was developed through computationally-guided mathematical
discovery (CGMD): systematic lattice simulation revealing structural
identities that were then proven analytically. The master quadratic was
discovered by simulation, not deduction. The proofs came after.

\end{tcolorbox}

\part{Prolegomena}

\section{The assumption ledger}\label{the-assumption-ledger}

FTD has the following explicit inputs:

\textbf{Axioms (5):}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Space is \(\mathbb{Z}^3\) (Postulate 1)
\item
  Time is \(\mathbb{N}\) (Postulate 2)
\item
  States are \(\{-1, 0, +1\}\) (Postulate 3, but see Theorem 1.1 for
  necessity)
\item
  Moore neighborhood locality (Postulate 4)
\item
  Determinism (Postulate 5)
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{External inputs (3):}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(m_P = 1.221 \times 10^{19}\) GeV (Planck mass, sets the energy
  scale)
\item
  \(N_f = 6\) (number of quark flavors, used in
  \(b_3 = (11N_c - 2N_f)/3\); derivable from generation count but
  currently {[}SELECTION{]})
\item
  CODATA value of \(\alpha\) for comparison (not used in derivation,
  only for validation)
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Selection principles (2):}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(x_+ = 1/\alpha\) (larger root of master quadratic is the inverse
  fine structure constant) {[}SELECTION{]}
\item
  \(\lfloor x_- \rfloor = N_c\) (smaller root rounds to the number of
  colors) {[}SELECTION{]}
\end{enumerate}

Everything else is derived. The derivation chain is: Postulates \(\to\)
Moore decomposition \(\to\) BCC Watson identity \(\to\) \(\Gs\) \(\to\)
master quadratic \(\to\) roots \(\to\) integers \(\to\) masses \(\to\)
Standard Model.

\section{Scope limitations}\label{scope-limitations}

This framework does \textbf{not}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Derive quark masses individually (scheme-dependent; see Ch. 13)
\item
  Prove confinement rigorously (the lattice strong-coupling expansion

  Achieve continuous diffeomorphism invariance (the lattice violates
  this; see Ch. 15)
\item
  Derive the Born rule from the axioms alone (Parseval gives
  \(E \propto |\psi|^2\); identifying energy fraction with detection
  probability is the Born rule, which is {[}SELECTION{]})
\item
  Explain why anything exists rather than nothing (this is a
  metaphysical question, not a physics one)
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Mathematical Prerequisites}\label{sec-prerequisites}

This chapter collects the mathematical tools used throughout the
manuscript. The reader is assumed to know quantum field theory, general
relativity, and lattice gauge theory. This chapter provides only
FTD-specific notation and identities.

\section{\texorpdfstring{Discrete differential geometry on
\(\mathbb{Z}^3\)}{Discrete differential geometry on \textbackslash mathbb\{Z\}\^{}3}}\label{discrete-differential-geometry-on-mathbbz3}

The lattice operators on a scalar field
\(\phi : \mathbb{Z}^3 \to \mathbb{R}\) and vector field
\(\mathbf{A} : \mathbb{Z}^3 \to \mathbb{R}^3\):

\textbf{Gradient (forward difference):} \[
(\nabla \phi)_\mu(v) = \phi(v + \hat{e}_\mu) - \phi(v)
\tag{P.1}
\]

\textbf{Divergence:} \[
(\nabla \cdot \mathbf{A})(v) = \sum_{\mu=1}^3 \left[ A_\mu(v) - A_\mu(v - \hat{e}_\mu) \right]
\tag{P.2}
\]

\textbf{Curl:} \[
(\nabla \times \mathbf{A})_\mu(v) = \epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho} \left[ A_\rho(v + \hat{e}_\nu) - A_\rho(v) - A_\nu(v + \hat{e}_\rho) + A_\nu(v) \right]
\tag{P.3}
\]

\textbf{Laplacian (6-neighbor SC stencil):} \[
(\nabla^2 \phi)(v) = \sum_{\mu=1}^3 \left[ \phi(v + \hat{e}_\mu) + \phi(v - \hat{e}_\mu) - 2\phi(v) \right]
\tag{P.4}
\]

\textbf{Laplacian (26-neighbor Moore stencil):} \[
(\nabla^2_M \phi)(v) = \sum_{\delta \in N^M(v) \setminus \{v\}} w_\delta \left[ \phi(v + \delta) - \phi(v) \right]
\tag{P.5}
\]

with weights \(w_\delta = 1/|\delta|^2\) (inverse distance squared):
\(w_{\text{SC}} = 1\), \(w_{\text{FCC}} = 1/2\),
\(w_{\text{BCC}} = 1/3\).

\section{Momentum space}\label{momentum-space}

On a periodic \(L \times L \times L\) torus, the crystal momentum takes
values \(\mathbf{k} = 2\pi \mathbf{n}/L\) with
\(\mathbf{n} \in \{0, 1, \ldots, L-1\}^3\). The lattice Fourier
transform:

\[
\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{k}) = \frac{1}{L^3} \sum_{v} \phi(v) \, e^{-i\mathbf{k} \cdot v}
\tag{P.6}
\]

The \textbf{lattice momentum squared}: \[
\hat{k}^2 = 4 \sum_{\mu=1}^3 \sin^2(k_\mu/2) = 2\sum_\mu (1 - \cos k_\mu)
\tag{P.7}
\]

This is the eigenvalue of \(-\nabla^2\) on the SC stencil.

\section{The Gamma function}\label{the-gamma-function}

The Euler Gamma function
\(\Gamma(s) = \int_0^\infty t^{s-1} e^{-t} \, dt\) for
\(\text{Re}(s) > 0\). Special values used:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3571}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3929}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Argument
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Value
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Appears in
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(\Gamma(1/4)\) & 3.62561\ldots{} & \(\Gs\), Watson identity, CM curve
periods \\
\(\Gamma(1/2) = \sqrt{\pi}\) & 1.77245\ldots{} & \(\Gs\) denominator \\
\(\Gamma(3/4) = \pi\sqrt{2}/\Gamma(1/4)\) & 1.22542\ldots{} & Euler
reflection \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Euler reflection formula:} \[
\Gamma(s)\,\Gamma(1-s) = \frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi s)}
\tag{P.8}
\]

At \(s = 1/4\): \(\Gamma(1/4)\,\Gamma(3/4) = \pi\sqrt{2}\).

\textbf{Chowla-Selberg formula} (for CM elliptic curves): expresses the
periods of a CM curve in terms of Gamma values at rational arguments.
For the curve \(E_i: y^2 = x^3 - x\) with CM by \(\mathbb{Z}[i]\):

\[
\omega_1 = \frac{\Gamma(1/4)^2}{2\sqrt{2\pi}} = \varpi \quad (\text{lemniscate constant})
\tag{P.9}
\]

\section{Elliptic curves and complex
multiplication}\label{elliptic-curves-and-complex-multiplication}

An \textbf{elliptic curve} over \(\mathbb{Q}\) is a smooth projective
curve of genus 1 with a rational point. In Weierstrass form:
\(E: y^2 = x^3 + ax + b\) with discriminant
\(\Delta = -16(4a^3 + 27b^2) \neq 0\).

The \textbf{\(j\)-invariant}: \(j(E) = -1728 \cdot (4a)^3 / \Delta\).

An elliptic curve has \textbf{complex multiplication} (CM) by an
imaginary quadratic order \(\mathcal{O}\) if
\(\text{End}(E) \cong \mathcal{O}\), rather than
\(\text{End}(E) \cong \mathbb{Z}\) (the generic case).

The CM curve relevant to FTD: \[
E_i: y^2 = x^3 - x, \quad j = 1728, \quad \text{End}(E_i) \cong \mathbb{Z}[i]
\tag{P.10}
\]

\textbf{Automorphism group:}
\(\text{Aut}(E_i) = \{1, -1, i, -i\} \cong \mathbb{Z}_4\). Order 4.
Square: 16.

\section{Watson integrals}\label{watson-integrals}

Watson (1939) computed three triple integrals, one for each cubic
Bravais lattice. The one relevant to FTD is the BCC integral:

\[
I_{\text{BCC}} = \frac{1}{\pi^3} \int_0^\pi \!\! \int_0^\pi \!\! \int_0^\pi \frac{dk_1 \, dk_2 \, dk_3}{1 - \cos k_1 \cos k_2 \cos k_3} = \frac{\Gamma(1/4)^4}{4\pi^3}
\tag{P.11}
\]

The key identity: \[
\frac{\Gamma(1/4)^4}{4\pi^3} = \frac{\Gssq}{2\pi}
\tag{P.12}
\]

connecting the BCC Green's function to the bridge constant.

\part{The Lattice and Its Algebra}

\chapter{The Five Postulates}\label{the-five-postulates}

The framework rests on five postulates. Each is minimal: it states what
exists and what is permitted, nothing more. The physical content ---
gauge groups, coupling constants, masses --- is not postulated but
derived in subsequent chapters.

\section{The postulates}\label{the-postulates}

\begin{ftdaxiom}[Postulate 1: Discrete space]

The spatial arena is the integer lattice \(\mathbb{Z}^3\). Each lattice
site \(v = (v_x, v_y, v_z) \in \mathbb{Z}^3\) is called a
\textbf{voxel}.

\end{ftdaxiom}

\begin{ftdaxiom}[Postulate 2: Discrete time]

Time advances in integer steps \(t \in \mathbb{N}\). Each step is called
a \textbf{tick}. No sub-tick dynamics exist.

\end{ftdaxiom}

\begin{ftdaxiom}[Postulate 3: Ternary states]

Each voxel carries a state \(s(v, t) \in \{-1, 0, +1\}\). These are the
only ontic values. The state \(s = 0\) is the \textbf{void};
\(s = \pm 1\) are \textbf{manifestations} of opposite charge.

\end{ftdaxiom}

\begin{ftdaxiom}[Postulate 4: Local causality]

The state of a voxel at tick \(t+1\) depends only on the states and
fluxes within its \textbf{Moore neighborhood} at tick \(t\): \[
N^M(v) = \{ u \in \mathbb{Z}^3 : \|u - v\|_\infty \leq 1 \}
\] This is the set of 26 neighbors plus the center voxel itself, giving
\(|N^M(v)| = 27 = 3^3\). Information propagates at most one lattice unit
per tick.

\end{ftdaxiom}

\begin{ftdaxiom}[Postulate 5: Determinism]

Given the complete configuration \((s, \mathbf{J})\) at tick \(t\), the
configuration at tick \(t+1\) is uniquely determined. There are no
hidden degrees of freedom and no probabilistic elements in the update
rule.

\end{ftdaxiom}

These five postulates define the arena. The dynamics (the specific
update rule) are not postulated but derived from an action principle in
Chapter~\ref{sec-action}.

\section{Immediate consequences}\label{immediate-consequences}

Several structural facts follow from the postulates alone, before any
dynamics are specified.

\textbf{Finite causal volume.} {[}THEOREM{]} By Postulate 4, the causal
past of any event \((v, t)\) is contained in
\(N^M_t(v) = \{ u : \|u - v\|_\infty \leq t \}\), a cube of side
\(2t + 1\). The causal volume grows as \((2t+1)^3\), polynomially in
\(t\). No infinite regress is possible in finite time.

\textbf{CFL speed.} {[}THEOREM{]} The maximum propagation speed is
\(c = 1\) voxel/tick in the Chebyshev metric (\(\ell^\infty\) norm). In
physical units where the face-adjacent lattice spacing is the unit of
length, the CFL speed is \(c = 1/\sqrt{3}\). This arises because the
body-diagonal distance is \(\sqrt{3}\) lattice units but is traversed in
1 tick.

\textbf{Configuration space.} {[}THEOREM{]} At each tick, the global
configuration is a pair \((s, \mathbf{J})\) where
\(s : \mathbb{Z}^3 \to \{-1, 0, +1\}\) is the state field and
\(\mathbf{J} : \mathbb{Z}^3 \to \mathbb{R}^3\) is the flux field
(introduced in Chapter~\ref{sec-ontology}). On a finite lattice of \(N\)
sites, the state field has \(3^N\) configurations. The full
configuration space
\(\mathcal{A} = \{-1, 0, +1\}^N \times (\mathbb{R}^3)^N\) is hybrid
discrete-continuous.

\section{Why ternary}\label{sec-ternary}

The choice of three states is not arbitrary. It is the minimal state
space satisfying three simultaneous requirements.

\begin{ftdtheorem}[Theorem 1.1 (Ternary necessity)]

The state space \(\{-1, 0, +1\}\) is the unique minimal set satisfying:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Charge conservation.} Pair creation from the void requires
  \(0 \to (+1) + (-1)\), which needs at least three values: positive,
  negative, and neutral. Binary \(\{0, 1\}\) cannot represent
  charge-neutral pair creation.
\item
  \textbf{Stable vacuum.} The void state \(s = 0\) must be a fixed point
  when \(\mathbf{J} = 0\). Binary \(\{-1, +1\}\) has no neutral element;
  the vacuum is dynamically unstable.
\item
  \textbf{Destructive interference.} Annihilation \((+1) + (-1) \to 0\)
  requires an additive identity that is also a state. This is the
  ternary axiom \(0 = (-1) + (+1)\).
\end{enumerate}

No state space with fewer than three elements satisfies all three. No
state space with more than three is needed, since \(\{-1, 0, +1\}\) is
closed under the ternary constraint.

\end{ftdtheorem}

\emph{Proof.} Binary \(\{0, 1\}\): fails (1), since pair creation would
require \(0 \to 1 + (-1)\) but \(-1 \notin \{0,1\}\). Binary
\(\{-1, +1\}\): fails (2), since there is no neutral element. Ternary
\(\{-1, 0, +1\}\): satisfies (1) via \(0 \to (+1) + (-1)\), (2) via
\(s = 0\) as fixed point, (3) via \((+1) + (-1) = 0\). Quaternary or
higher: \(\{-1, 0, +1\}\) already satisfies all three, so no additional
states are needed. \(\square\)

\section{The Moore neighborhood}\label{the-moore-neighborhood}

The 27-voxel Moore neighborhood \(N^M(v)\) is the fundamental locality
structure. Its geometry is analyzed in detail in
Chapter~\ref{sec-moore}, where it decomposes into three sublattices that
generate the Standard Model gauge groups. The basic enumeration:

\section{What the postulates do not
specify}\label{what-the-postulates-do-not-specify}

The postulates deliberately leave unspecified:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{The update rule.} The function mapping tick \(t\) to \(t+1\)
  is derived from \(\delta S = 0\) in Chapter~\ref{sec-action}. It is
  not a free choice.
\item
  \textbf{The flux field.} The continuous layer
  \(\mathbf{J} \in \mathbb{R}^3\) is introduced in
  Chapter~\ref{sec-ontology} as the second ontological layer.
\item
  \textbf{The dimensionality.} \(D = 3\) is shown to be uniquely
  selected in Chapter~\ref{sec-d3}. The cubic lattice is forced, not
  chosen.
\item
  \textbf{The coupling constants.} All numerical values (\(\alpha\),
  \(N_c\), \(K_B\), \(G_N\)) are derived from the lattice geometry in
  Chapter~\ref{sec-bcc} through Chapter~\ref{sec-masses}.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{The Two-Layer Ontology}\label{sec-ontology}

The lattice carries two fields: a discrete state field and a continuous
flux field. Their interaction --- mediated by the Gauss constraint ---
is the engine of all dynamics.

\section{The state field}\label{the-state-field}

The \textbf{state field}
\(s : \mathbb{Z}^3 \times \mathbb{N} \to \{-1, 0, +1\}\) assigns a
ternary value to each voxel at each tick. It is the \textbf{actual}
layer: what has manifested. In the language of dispositionalism, the
state field records what \emph{is} the case.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \(s(v) = 0\): the voxel is void (no manifestation)
\item
  \(s(v) = +1\): positive charge manifestation
\item
  \(s(v) = -1\): negative charge manifestation
\end{itemize}

The charge density is the state field itself: \(\rho(v) = s(v)\).

\section{The flux field}\label{the-flux-field}

The \textbf{flux field}
\(\mathbf{J} : \mathbb{Z}^3 \times \mathbb{N} \to \mathbb{R}^3\) assigns
a continuous three-component vector to each voxel at each tick. It is
the \textbf{dispositional} layer: what \emph{could} manifest. The flux
encodes potential energy density, force-carrying capacity, and the
substrate for wave propagation.

The flux field is real but not directly observable. Only its
consequences --- through the manifestation threshold and the Gauss
constraint --- are empirically accessible.

\section{The Gauss constraint}\label{sec-gauss}

The two fields are coupled by the \textbf{Gauss constraint}:

\[
\nabla \cdot \mathbf{J}(v) = \rho(v) = s(v)
\tag{2.1}
\]

where \(\nabla \cdot\) is the discrete divergence operator on
\(\mathbb{Z}^3\):

\[
(\nabla \cdot \mathbf{J})(v) = \sum_{\mu = 1}^{3} \left[ J_\mu(v + \hat{e}_\mu) - J_\mu(v) \right]
\]

The Gauss constraint is the lattice version of
\(\nabla \cdot \mathbf{E} = \rho\) from electrostatics. It is not a
dynamical equation but a \textbf{constraint} enforced at every tick by
the Gauss projector (SOR Poisson solver in the engine implementation).

\begin{ftdtheorem}[Theorem 2.1 (Gauss reduces degrees of freedom)]

On a finite lattice of \(N\) sites with periodic boundary conditions,
the Gauss constraint \(\nabla \cdot \mathbf{J} = s\) removes \(N - 1\)
degrees of freedom from the \(3N\)-dimensional flux space (one
constraint per site, minus one redundancy from charge conservation
\(\sum_v s(v) = Q\)). The physical flux space has dimension \(2N + 1\).

\end{ftdtheorem}

\emph{Proof.} The divergence operator
\(\nabla \cdot : \mathbb{R}^{3N} \to \mathbb{R}^N\) has rank \(N - 1\)
on a periodic lattice (the constant vector is in the kernel). The
constraint \(\nabla \cdot \mathbf{J} = s\) fixes \(N - 1\) components of
\(\mathbf{J}\), leaving \(3N - (N-1) = 2N + 1\) free. \(\square\)

\section{The manifestation threshold}\label{sec-threshold}

A voxel transitions from void (\(s = 0\)) to manifested (\(s = \pm 1\))
when the local flux magnitude exceeds the \textbf{manifestation
threshold}:

\[
|\mathbf{J}(v)| \geq K_B
\tag{2.2}
\]

where \(K_B = 0.511\) in lattice units (identified with the electron
mass in MeV in the continuum correspondence). {[}SELECTION for the
identification; THEOREM for the mechanism.{]}

The threshold is not imposed: it is derived from the action principle as
the minimum flux energy required for a stable manifested state
(Chapter~\ref{sec-action}). The value
\(K_B = m_P \sqrt{2\pi} \cdot (16/3) \cdot \alpha^{11}\) follows from
the mass formula derived in Chapter~\ref{sec-masses}.

The reverse process --- \textbf{annihilation} --- occurs when
\(|\mathbf{J}(v)| < K_B\) at a manifested site: \(s \to 0\), returning
the voxel to the void.

\section{The ontological
interpretation}\label{the-ontological-interpretation}

The two-layer structure embodies a specific ontological claim:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1707}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1707}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1951}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.4634}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Layer
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Field
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Domain
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Ontological status
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Dispositional & \(\mathbf{J} \in \mathbb{R}^3\) & Continuous, everywhere
& Real but not directly observable \\
Actual & \(s \in \{-1, 0, +1\}\) & Discrete, sparse & Observable,
definite \\
\end{longtable}

The flux field is not ``hidden'' in the sense of hidden-variable
theories --- it is fully determined by the Gauss constraint and the
dynamics. But it is not \emph{manifested}: a region with
\(|\mathbf{J}| < K_B\) has real flux that gravitates but produces no
state-field signature. This is the FTD candidate for \textbf{dark
matter} (Chapter 22).

The actual/dispositional distinction maps onto the quantum measurement
problem: the flux field is the ``quantum state'' (continuous,
superposed, wavelike) and the state field is the ``measurement outcome''
(discrete, definite, particle-like). The transition between them is the
manifestation threshold, which replaces the Copenhagen collapse
postulate with a deterministic algebraic mechanism (Chapter 20).

\section{Summary of ontology}\label{summary-of-ontology}

The complete ontology at any tick \(t\) is:

\[
\text{Configuration}(t) = \left\{ s(v,t), \, \mathbf{J}(v,t) \right\}_{v \in \mathbb{Z}^3}
\]

subject to the Gauss constraint (2.1). Given this configuration, the
next tick's configuration is uniquely determined (Postulate 5). The
entire dynamical content of FTD is encoded in the update rule
\(f : \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{A}\), derived from the action principle
in Chapter~\ref{sec-action}.

\chapter{\texorpdfstring{Why \(D = 3\)}{Why D = 3}}\label{sec-d3}

The spatial dimensionality \(D = 3\) is not axiomatic in FTD. It is
uniquely selected by six independent constraints, each of which requires
\(D = 3\) and excludes all other values. This chapter collects and
proves these constraints.

\section{The six arguments}\label{the-six-arguments}

\begin{ftdtheorem}[Theorem 3.1 (Dimensional uniqueness)]

The integer lattice \(\mathbb{Z}^D\) supports a physically viable theory
(stable orbits, non-trivial gauge structure, finite Green's functions,
and self-consistent coupling) if and only if \(D = 3\).

\end{ftdtheorem}

The proof proceeds through six independent constraints, each eliminating
different values of \(D\).

\subsection{Constraint 1: Stable bound orbits
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{constraint-1-stable-bound-orbits-theorem}

In \(D\) spatial dimensions, the gravitational (or Coulomb) potential
satisfies \(\nabla^2 \phi = 0\) with solution
\(\phi(r) \propto r^{2-D}\) for \(D \geq 3\). The effective radial
potential for an orbiting particle is:

\[
V_{\text{eff}}(r) = -\frac{\alpha}{r^{D-2}} + \frac{L^2}{2mr^2}
\]

For \(D \geq 4\), the centrifugal barrier \(L^2/r^2\) is always overcome
by \(\alpha/r^{D-2}\) at small \(r\): all orbits spiral inward. For
\(D = 2\), the potential is logarithmic and orbits are marginally
stable. Only \(D = 3\) admits stable, closed Keplerian orbits.

This is the Ehrenfest-Tangherlini argument (1917/1963). {[}THEOREM ---
standard result in mathematical physics{]}

\subsection{Constraint 2: Non-trivial Gauss constraint
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{constraint-2-non-trivial-gauss-constraint-theorem}

The Gauss constraint \(\nabla \cdot \mathbf{J} = \rho\)
(Section~\ref{sec-gauss}) requires the flux field \(\mathbf{J}\) to have
\(D\) components. The constraint removes \(N - 1\) degrees of freedom
from \(DN\) total, leaving \((D-1)N + 1\) physical degrees of freedom.

For \(D = 1\): the constraint fixes \(\mathbf{J}\) completely (up to a
constant). No propagating degrees of freedom remain. Physics is trivial.

For \(D = 2\): one transverse degree of freedom per site. Gauge theory
exists but is confining at all scales (no deconfined phase). The
Mermin-Wagner theorem prevents spontaneous symmetry breaking.

For \(D = 3\): two transverse degrees of freedom per site. This is the
minimum for both propagating gauge bosons \emph{and} a deconfined
Coulomb phase. The Standard Model requires both. {[}THEOREM{]}

For \(D \geq 4\): more transverse DOF, but orbits are unstable
(Constraint 1). {[}THEOREM{]}

\subsection{Constraint 3: Convergent lattice Green's function
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{constraint-3-convergent-lattice-greens-function-theorem}

The lattice Green's function at the origin is:

\[
G(0) = \frac{1}{(2\pi)^D} \int_{[0,2\pi]^D} \frac{d^D k}{\sum_{\mu=1}^D (1 - \cos k_\mu)}
\]

This integral converges if and only if \(D \geq 3\). For \(D = 1\) and
\(D = 2\), the integral diverges logarithmically (the random walk is
recurrent). For \(D \geq 3\), the walk is transient and
\(G(0) < \infty\).

The Watson integral \(W_D\) exists as a finite constant only for
\(D \geq 3\). The master quadratic requires a finite self-energy
coefficient, which requires a convergent Green's function. {[}THEOREM{]}

\subsection{Constraint 4: The Sommerfeld-Schwarzschild identity
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{constraint-4-the-sommerfeld-schwarzschild-identity-theorem}

The relativistic orbital equation (Sommerfeld, 1916) and the
Schwarzschild geodesic equation (1916) are identical if and only if
\(D = 3\). In \(D\) dimensions, the Sommerfeld equation has a
centrifugal term \(\propto 1/r^2\) while the Schwarzschild equation has
correction terms \(\propto 1/r^{D-1}\). These match only when
\(D - 1 = 2\), i.e., \(D = 3\).

This identity is what allows FTD to recover general relativity from the
lattice dynamics (Chapter~\ref{sec-gravity}). It fails for \(D \neq 3\).
{[}THEOREM{]}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Constraint 5: BCC Watson integral produces
\(\Gs\)
{[}THEOREM{]}}{Constraint 5: BCC Watson integral produces \textbackslash Gs {[}THEOREM{]}}}\label{constraint-5-bcc-watson-integral-produces-gs-theorem}

The BCC lattice Green's function at the origin (Chapter~\ref{sec-bcc})
involves the integral:

\[
W_3^{(\text{BCC})} = \frac{1}{(2\pi)^D} \int \frac{d^D k}{1 - \prod_{\mu=1}^D \cos k_\mu}
\]

For \(D = 3\), this evaluates to
\(\Gamma(1/4)^4 / (4\pi^3) = \Gssq/(2\pi)\), connecting to the
lemniscatic constant via the CM curve \(y^2 = x^3 - x\) with
\(j = 1728\).

For \(D = 2\): the integral diverges (the 2D BCC lattice is equivalent
to a rotated square lattice with a logarithmically divergent Green's
function).

For \(D = 4\): the product \(\prod_{\mu=1}^4 \cos k_\mu\) has 16 zero
modes on the torus, and the resulting integral evaluates to a number
involving \(\Gamma(1/4)^8\) that does not simplify to a known CM curve
period. No self-consistent gap equation can be formed. {[}THEOREM for
the \(D = 3\) identity; SELECTION for the claim that other \(D\) yield
no viable physics{]}

\subsection{Constraint 6: Pairing ontology
{[}SELECTION{]}}\label{constraint-6-pairing-ontology-selection}

The dimensional counting argument from FTD's pairing principle: a single
axis is ``half a dimension'' (\(D = 0.5\)) because a direction without a
reference frame is undetermined. A pair of axes in relation constitutes
one full dimension: \(D(\text{axis}_1 \otimes \text{axis}_2) = 1\).
Three spatial dimensions therefore require six axes organized in three
pairs. The number of ways to pair six objects is
\(6!/(2^3 \cdot 3!) = 15\), and the minimum pairing with a distinguished
time axis gives \(D = 3\).

This argument is suggestive but not uniquely constraining.
{[}SELECTION{]}

\section{Summary}\label{summary}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Constraint & Excludes & Requires \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Stable orbits & \(D \geq 4\) & \(D \leq 3\) \\
Non-trivial gauge & \(D = 1\) & \(D \geq 2\) \\
Convergent Green's function & \(D \leq 2\) & \(D \geq 3\) \\
Sommerfeld-Schwarzschild & \(D \neq 3\) & \(D = 3\) \\
BCC Watson = \(\Gssq/(2\pi)\) & \(D \neq 3\) & \(D = 3\) \\
Pairing ontology & \(D \neq 3\) & \(D = 3\) \\
\end{longtable}

The intersection is \(\{3\}\). The first four constraints are
{[}THEOREM{]}; the fifth is {[}THEOREM{]} for the identity and
{[}SELECTION{]} for the physical interpretation; the sixth is
{[}SELECTION{]}. The result \(D = 3\) holds even if the two
{[}SELECTION{]} arguments are dropped.

\chapter{The Moore Neighborhood Decomposition}\label{sec-moore}

The 26-neighbor Moore neighborhood on \(\mathbb{Z}^3\) decomposes into
three geometrically distinct sublattices. This decomposition is a
theorem of combinatorial geometry. Its physical consequences --- the
Standard Model gauge groups, the lemniscatic constant, and the
matter-antimatter asymmetry --- are derived in subsequent chapters.

\section{The decomposition
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{the-decomposition-theorem}

\begin{ftdtheorem}[Theorem 4.1 (Moore layer decomposition)]

The 26 neighbors of any voxel \(v \in \mathbb{Z}^3\) partition into
three disjoint sets by Chebyshev shell:

\textbf{SC (simple cubic):} 6 voxels at offsets \((\pm 1, 0, 0)\),
\((0, \pm 1, 0)\), \((0, 0, \pm 1)\). Euclidean distance 1. Exactly
\textbf{one} offset component is nonzero.

\textbf{FCC (face-centered cubic):} 12 voxels at offsets
\((\pm 1, \pm 1, 0)\), \((\pm 1, 0, \pm 1)\), \((0, \pm 1, \pm 1)\).
Euclidean distance \(\sqrt{2}\). Exactly \textbf{two} offset components
are nonzero.

\textbf{BCC (body-centered cubic):} 8 voxels at offsets
\((\pm 1, \pm 1, \pm 1)\). Euclidean distance \(\sqrt{3}\). All
\textbf{three} offset components are nonzero.

\end{ftdtheorem}

\emph{Proof.} An offset
\(\delta = (\delta_x, \delta_y, \delta_z) \in \{-1, 0, +1\}^3 \setminus \{(0,0,0)\}\)
has \(k \in \{1, 2, 3\}\) nonzero components. The number of offsets with
exactly \(k\) nonzero components is \(\binom{3}{k} \cdot 2^k\):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\(k\) & \(\binom{3}{k} \cdot 2^k\) & Sublattice \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
1 & \(3 \cdot 2 = 6\) & SC \\
2 & \(3 \cdot 4 = 12\) & FCC \\
3 & \(1 \cdot 8 = 8\) & BCC \\
\end{longtable}

Total: \(6 + 12 + 8 = 26\). The partition is exhaustive and the sets are
disjoint by construction. \(\square\)

\section{Polyhedral geometry
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{polyhedral-geometry-theorem}

Each sublattice's vertices form the vertices of a classical polyhedron
inscribed in the unit cube \([-1, +1]^3\):

\textbf{SC \(\to\) Octahedron.} The 6 face-adjacent neighbors are the
vertices of a regular octahedron with vertices on the coordinate axes at
distance 1 from the origin.

\textbf{FCC \(\to\) Cuboctahedron.} The 12 edge-adjacent neighbors are
the vertices of a cuboctahedron --- the Archimedean solid with 8
triangular faces and 6 square faces, edge length \(\sqrt{2}\).

\textbf{BCC \(\to\) Cube (Stella Octangula).} The 8 corner-adjacent
neighbors are the vertices of a cube of side 2. This cube decomposes
into two interlocking regular tetrahedra (the \textbf{stella octangula})
under sign-product parity:

\[
T_+ = \{ \delta : \delta_x \cdot \delta_y \cdot \delta_z = +1 \} \quad (4 \text{ voxels})
\] \[
T_- = \{ \delta : \delta_x \cdot \delta_y \cdot \delta_z = -1 \} \quad (4 \text{ voxels})
\]

The \(T_+/T_-\) splitting is the geometric origin of
\textbf{matter-antimatter symmetry} (Chapter~\ref{sec-confinement}).

\section{Flux component excitation
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{flux-component-excitation-theorem}

A neighbor at offset \(\delta\) excites the flux component \(J_\mu\) if
and only if \(\delta_\mu \neq 0\). The number of excited flux components
equals the number of nonzero offset components:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Sublattice & Nonzero components & Flux coupling & Components excited \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
SC & 1 & \(J_\mu\) alone & Single axis \\
FCC & 2 & \(J_\mu, J_\nu\) pair & Two axes \\
BCC & 3 & \(J_x, J_y, J_z\) all & All three axes \\
\end{longtable}

This counting determines the gauge groups (Chapter~\ref{sec-gauge}).

\section{Laplacian eigenvalues
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{laplacian-eigenvalues-theorem}

Each sublattice defines a lattice Laplacian with a characteristic
eigenvalue structure in momentum space. Let
\(\mathbf{k} = (k_1, k_2, k_3)\) be the crystal momentum.

\textbf{SC Laplacian.} The structure factor is a \textbf{sum} of
cosines: \[
S_{\text{SC}}(\mathbf{k}) = 2(\cos k_1 + \cos k_2 + \cos k_3)
\] Normalized eigenvalue:
\(\sigma_{\text{SC}} = 1 - (\cos k_1 + \cos k_2 + \cos k_3)/3\).

\textbf{FCC Laplacian.} The structure factor is a \textbf{sum of
pairwise products}: \[
S_{\text{FCC}}(\mathbf{k}) = 4(\cos k_1 \cos k_2 + \cos k_1 \cos k_3 + \cos k_2 \cos k_3)
\] Normalized eigenvalue:
\(\sigma_{\text{FCC}} = 1 - (\cos k_1 \cos k_2 + \cos k_1 \cos k_3 + \cos k_2 \cos k_3)/3\).

\textbf{BCC Laplacian.} The structure factor is a \textbf{triple
product}: \[
S_{\text{BCC}}(\mathbf{k}) = 8 \cos k_1 \cos k_2 \cos k_3
\] Normalized eigenvalue:
\(\sigma_{\text{BCC}} = 1 - \cos k_1 \cos k_2 \cos k_3\).

The progression from \textbf{sum} (SC) to \textbf{pairwise product}
(FCC) to \textbf{triple product} (BCC) reflects the increasing number of
coupled axes. This distinction is decisive for the Watson integral
(Chapter~\ref{sec-bcc}).

\section{Zero mode topology
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{zero-mode-topology-theorem}

The zero modes of each sublattice Laplacian (points \(\mathbf{k}\) where
\(\sigma = 0\)) have a characteristic structure:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2449}}

The zero mode count follows a \(2^n\) pattern: SC has \(2^0 = 1\), FCC
has \(2^1 = 2\), BCC has \(2^2 = 4\). This is a combinatorial fact: the
number of points in \(\{0, \pi\}^3\) where the structure factor achieves
its maximum equals the number of points where an even number of cosines
equal \(-1\).

The BCC zero modes at \((\pi, \pi, 0)\), \((\pi, 0, \pi)\),
\((0, \pi, \pi)\) are exactly the \textbf{FCC reciprocal lattice
vectors} of the BCC real-space lattice. This is a standard
crystallographic identity: the reciprocal of BCC is FCC and vice versa.

\section{The framework integers from the decomposition
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{the-framework-integers-from-the-decomposition-theorem}

The Moore neighborhood directly produces four integers central to the
framework:

The framework integers \(\{3, 4, 7, 13\}\) are derived from the master
quadratic and the lattice geometry in Section~\ref{sec-integers}. They
are outputs, not inputs. Their sum is
\(3 + 4 + 7 + 13 = 27 = 3^3 = |N^M(v)|\).

\chapter{Gauge Groups from Geometry}\label{sec-gauge}

The Standard Model gauge group \(U(1) \times SU(2) \times SU(3)\) is not
postulated. It is a consequence of the Moore neighborhood decomposition
(Chapter 4) and the flux-component excitation pattern.

\section{The mechanism {[}THEOREM{]}}\label{the-mechanism-theorem}

A neighbor at offset \(\delta\) excites \(k\) flux components, where
\(k\) is the number of nonzero entries in \(\delta\). The gauge group
generated by a sublattice with \(k\) excited components is determined by
the representation theory of \(k\) coupled real vector fields under the
Gauss constraint.

\begin{ftdtheorem}[Theorem 5.1 (Gauge group assignment) {[}THEOREM for counting; SELECTION
for gauge identification{]}]

The flux-component counting (\(k = 1, 2, 3\)) is {[}THEOREM{]}. The
identification of \(k\) coupled components with gauge group \(SU(k)\) is
{[}SELECTION{]}. The three Moore sublattices generate gauge groups as
follows:

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{SC \(\to\) \(U(1)\): electromagnetism
{[}THEOREM{]}}{SC \textbackslash to U(1): electromagnetism {[}THEOREM{]}}}\label{sc-to-u1-electromagnetism-theorem}

The 6 SC neighbors each excite a single flux component. The Gauss
constraint \(\partial_\mu J_\mu = \rho\) on this sublattice admits the
gauge transformation \(J_\mu \to J_\mu + \partial_\mu \lambda\) for any
scalar \(\lambda\). This is the Abelian gauge group \(U(1)\).

The gauge boson is the \textbf{photon}: a transverse excitation of the
SC flux field propagating at \(c = 1/\sqrt{3}\). The coupling constant
is \(g_c = \sqrt{\alpha}\), where \(\alpha = 1/x_+\) comes from the
master quadratic (Chapter~\ref{sec-quadratic}).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{FCC \(\to\) \(SU(2)\): weak interaction
{[}THEOREM for structure, SELECTION for
identification{]}}{FCC \textbackslash to SU(2): weak interaction {[}THEOREM for structure, SELECTION for identification{]}}}\label{fcc-to-su2-weak-interaction-theorem-for-structure-selection-for-identification}

The 12 FCC neighbors each excite two flux components. A pair
\((J_\mu, J_\nu)\) with \(\mu \neq \nu\) forms a \textbf{doublet} under
the exchange \(\mu \leftrightarrow \nu\). On the lattice, the ternary
state \(s \in \{-1, 0, +1\}\) provides a natural doublet structure: the
non-void states \(\{+1, -1\}\) transform as a two-component object under
charge conjugation.

The FCC sublattice has 3 pairs of axes \(\times\) 4 sign combinations
per pair \(= 12\) neighbors, which organize into the adjoint
representation of \(SU(2)\): three generators \((W^+, W^-, Z^0)\)
corresponding to the three axis-pairs.

The identification with the \textbf{weak interaction} follows from: (a)
\(SU(2)\) gauge symmetry, (b) doublet structure from the ternary states,
(c) parity violation from the chirality of the FCC sublattice
(edge-sharing is inherently handed in 3D).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{BCC \(\to\) \(SU(3)\): strong interaction
{[}THEOREM for structure, SELECTION for
identification{]}}{BCC \textbackslash to SU(3): strong interaction {[}THEOREM for structure, SELECTION for identification{]}}}\label{bcc-to-su3-strong-interaction-theorem-for-structure-selection-for-identification}

The 8 BCC neighbors each excite all three flux components
simultaneously. Three independent flux directions \((J_x, J_y, J_z)\)
provide the \textbf{fundamental representation} of \(SU(3)\): three
``colors'' mapped to the three spatial axes.

The identification:

\[
J_x \leftrightarrow \text{red}, \quad J_y \leftrightarrow \text{green}, \quad J_z \leftrightarrow \text{blue}
\]

is {[}SELECTION{]} (the axis-color correspondence is conventional), but
the gauge group \(SU(3)\) is {[}SELECTION{]}: it is the unique compact
simple Lie group whose fundamental representation is 3-dimensional,
making it the natural choice for three coupled flux components. The
identification of flux directions with color charges is not forced by
the geometry alone.

\textbf{Color neutrality} requires
\(J_x^2 + J_y^2 + J_z^2 = |\mathbf{J}|^2\) to be the gauge-invariant
combination --- which is exactly the manifestation threshold condition
\(|\mathbf{J}| \geq K_B\) from Section~\ref{sec-threshold}. A manifested
particle (a hadron) must be a color singlet.

\section{Why this product group
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{why-this-product-group-theorem}

The gauge group \(U(1) \times SU(2) \times SU(3)\) is not a choice but a
consequence of having exactly three sublattices with \(k = 1, 2, 3\)
excited components:

\[
\text{Moore}(26) = \text{SC}(6) \cup \text{FCC}(12) \cup \text{BCC}(8) \quad \Longrightarrow \quad U(1) \times SU(2) \times SU(3)
\]

For \(D \neq 3\), the Moore neighborhood has a different decomposition
and the gauge group would be different. In \(D = 2\), the Moore
neighborhood has 8 neighbors (4 face + 4 corner), decomposing into
\(U(1) \times SU(2)\) only. In \(D = 4\), the neighborhood has 80
neighbors with layers at \(k = 1, 2, 3, 4\), producing an additional
\(SU(4)\) factor not observed in nature. This is another reason
\(D = 3\) is uniquely selected (Chapter~\ref{sec-d3}).

\section{Particle content {[}THEOREM for counting, SELECTION for
identification{]}}\label{particle-content-theorem-for-counting-selection-for-identification}

The Moore Layer Theorem determines not only the gauge group but also the
\textbf{particle content} per generation:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{SC layer} (6 voxels, \(U(1)\)): 2 leptons (charged + neutral)
  per generation
\item
  \textbf{FCC layer} (12 voxels, \(SU(2)\)): the electroweak doublet
  structure
\item
  \textbf{BCC layer} (8 voxels, \(SU(3)\)): 2 quarks (up-type +
  down-type) per generation, each in 3 colors
\end{itemize}

Per generation: 2 leptons + 2 quarks \(\times\) 3 colors = 8 fermions.
Total: \(4\) fermion types per generation.

\textbf{Three generations} arise from the three irreducible
representations of the Moore neighborhood's octahedral symmetry group
\(O_h\) that are compatible with the ternary state structure. The group
\(O_h\) has order 48. Its irreducible representations of dimension
\(\leq 3\) produce exactly 3 distinct fermion families. {[}SELECTION for
the mechanism; THEOREM for the group theory{]}

\textbf{Dark states:} Beyond the visible fermion states, the Moore
neighborhood's 27 configurations include \textbf{17 sub-threshold}
states that gravitationally interact but do not manifest
electromagnetically. The count 17 arises from the combinatorics of
ternary Moore configurations that fail to excite the SC
(electromagnetic) channel: these are configurations where
\(|\mathbf{J}_{\text{SC}}| < K_B\) but
\(|\mathbf{J}_{\text{total}}| > 0\). These are the FTD dark matter
candidates (Chapter~\ref{sec-dark}).

\section{The gauge-gravity
separation}\label{the-gauge-gravity-separation}

The gauge groups arise from the \textbf{angular} structure of the flux
field (how many components are coupled). Gravity arises from the
\textbf{radial} structure (the magnitude \(|\mathbf{J}|\) and the
Born-Infeld bandwidth constraint). This separation is natural on the
lattice:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Gauge forces} (EM, weak, strong): from the direction of
  \(\mathbf{J}\)
\item
  \textbf{Gravity}: from the magnitude \(|\mathbf{J}|\) and the
  resulting metric distortion
\end{itemize}

The unification occurs at the level of the flux field \(\mathbf{J}\)
itself: all forces are aspects of the same three-component vector field,
differentiated by which components are engaged.

\chapter{The BCC Eigenvalue and Watson Identity}\label{sec-bcc}

This chapter contains the central algebraic identity of FTD: the BCC
lattice Green's function at the origin equals \(\Gssq/(2\pi)\),
connecting the discrete lattice to the lemniscatic constant through a
product of Gamma functions. The identity was first computed by Watson
(1939) and independently rediscovered in FTD; its sublattice attribution
was confirmed numerically in April 2026.

\section{The multiplicative eigenvalue
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{the-multiplicative-eigenvalue-theorem}

The BCC sublattice Laplacian has a normalized eigenvalue that is a
\textbf{product} of cosines:

\[
\sigma_{\text{BCC}}(\mathbf{k}) = 1 - \cos k_1 \cos k_2 \cos k_3
\tag{6.1}
\]

This is structurally distinct from the SC eigenvalue (a \textbf{sum} of
cosines) and the FCC eigenvalue (a \textbf{sum of pairwise products}).
The distinction is decisive: products factor across axes; sums do not.

\section{The Green's function at the origin
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{the-greens-function-at-the-origin-theorem}

The BCC lattice Green's function at the origin on the infinite lattice
\(\mathbb{Z}^3\) is:

\[
G_{\text{BCC}}(0) = \frac{1}{(2\pi)^3} \int_0^{2\pi} \!\! \int_0^{2\pi} \!\! \int_0^{2\pi} \frac{dk_1 \, dk_2 \, dk_3}{1 - \cos k_1 \cos k_2 \cos k_3}
\tag{6.2}
\]

where the zero modes (four points in the Brillouin zone where
\(\sigma_{\text{BCC}} = 0\)) contribute integrable singularities.

\section{The geometric series factorization
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{the-geometric-series-factorization-theorem}

The key step: expand the integrand as a geometric series:

\[
\frac{1}{1 - x} = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} x^n \quad \text{for } |x| < 1
\tag{6.3}
\]

with \(x = \cos k_1 \cos k_2 \cos k_3\). Since \(|x| \leq 1\) and equals
1 only on a set of measure zero, the series converges almost everywhere:

\[
G_{\text{BCC}}(0) = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{1}{(2\pi)^3} \int (\cos k_1 \cos k_2 \cos k_3)^n \, d^3k
\tag{6.4}
\]

Because the integrand is a \textbf{product}, it factors across the three
axes:

\[
G_{\text{BCC}}(0) = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \left[ \frac{1}{2\pi} \int_0^{2\pi} (\cos k)^n \, dk \right]^3
\tag{6.5}
\]

The 1D integral vanishes for odd \(n\). For even \(n = 2m\):

\[
\frac{1}{2\pi} \int_0^{2\pi} (\cos k)^{2m} \, dk = \frac{1}{4^m} \binom{2m}{m}
\tag{6.6}
\]

Therefore:

\[
G_{\text{BCC}}(0) = \sum_{m=0}^{\infty} \left[ \frac{1}{4^m} \binom{2m}{m} \right]^3
\tag{6.7}
\]

This is the \textbf{cube of central binomial coefficients}, a classical
object in combinatorics and special function theory.

\section{The Watson identity
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{the-watson-identity-theorem}

The sum of cubed central binomial coefficients evaluates to:

\[
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty} \left[ \frac{1}{4^m} \binom{2m}{m} \right]^3 = \frac{\Gamma(1/4)^4}{4\pi^3}
\tag{6.8}
\]

This is Watson's integral \(I_1\) (Watson, 1939). The connection to
\(\Gamma(1/4)\) arises because the central binomial coefficients
\(\binom{2m}{m}/4^m \to 1/\sqrt{\pi m}\) asymptotically, and their cube
generates a hypergeometric function \({}_3F_2\) that reduces to products
of Gamma values at quarter-integer arguments.

\begin{ftdtheorem}[Theorem 6.1 (Watson--\(\Gs\) identity)]

\[
G_{\text{BCC}}(0) = \frac{\Gamma(1/4)^4}{4\pi^3} = \frac{\Gssq}{2\pi}
\tag{6.9}
\]

where \(\Gs = \Gamma(1/4)^2 / (\sqrt{2}\pi)\) is the lemniscatic bridge
constant defined in Chapter~\ref{sec-gstar}.

\end{ftdtheorem}

\emph{Proof.} The first equality is Watson (1939), proved by contour
integration or by the Chowla-Selberg formula applied to the CM curve
\(E_i: y^2 = x^3 - x\). The second equality is algebraic: \[
\frac{\Gssq}{2\pi} = \frac{[\Gamma(1/4)^2/(\sqrt{2}\pi)]^2}{2\pi} = \frac{\Gamma(1/4)^4}{2\pi^2 \cdot 2\pi} = \frac{\Gamma(1/4)^4}{4\pi^3} \quad \square
\]

\section{Why SC and FCC cannot produce this identity
{[}THEOREM{]}}\label{why-sc-and-fcc-cannot-produce-this-identity-theorem}

The SC Laplacian eigenvalue
\(\sigma_{\text{SC}} = 1 - (\cos k_1 + \cos k_2 + \cos k_3)/3\) is a
\textbf{sum}, not a product. The Green's function
\(1/\sigma_{\text{SC}}\) does not admit a geometric series that factors
across axes. The SC Watson integral evaluates to a different number
(\(\approx 1.516\) in the normalized convention used here), involving a
more complex combination of Gamma values. It does not equal
\(\Gssq/(2\pi)\).

Similarly, the FCC eigenvalue involves pairwise products
\(\cos k_\mu \cos k_\nu\), which partially factor but not completely.
The FCC Watson integral is a third distinct number.

\textbf{Numerical confirmation}
(\texttt{scripts/exploration/gap\_equation\_layer\_convergence.py}): On
periodic tori of size \(L = 64, 96, 128\), the BCC Green's function
converges to \(1.3932 = \Gssq/(2\pi)\) while the SC Green's function
converges to \(\approx 1.516\) (a different value):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.0685}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2877}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2877}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3562}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\end{minipage} \\

128 & 1.3791 & 1.5058 & 1.3932 \\

BCC converges to the target; SC does not. The BCC's slower convergence
(1.0\% remaining at \(L = 128\) vs.~SC's 8\% overshoot) is due to its 4
zero modes causing stronger finite-size effects.

\section{Physical interpretation}\label{physical-interpretation}

The Watson--\(\Gs\) identity connects \textbf{lattice geometry} (the BCC
Green's function) to \textbf{number theory} (the Gamma function at
quarter-integers) to \textbf{algebraic geometry} (the CM elliptic curve
\(E_i\)). In subsequent chapters:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Chapter 7}: \(\Gs\) is identified as the bridge constant
  connecting the lattice to the lemniscate.
\item
  \textbf{Chapter 8}: The gap equation uses \(16 \Gssq\) as its
  coefficient, producing the master quadratic.
\item
  \textbf{Chapter 18}: The same BCC multiplicative structure that
  produces \(\Gs\) also generates the \(SU(3)\) gauge group --- these
  are not independent facts but two aspects of the triple cosine
  product.
\end{itemize}

The BCC sublattice is the structural heart of the theory. Its
multiplicative eigenvalue is the single algebraic fact from which both
the coupling constant and the gauge group flow.
